### [The separation principle: where do beliefs and desires come from?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/awMDNhoL6J97s6wFJ/the-separation-principle-where-do-beliefs-and-desires-come)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><b><span>TLDR</span></b></i><i><span>: Psychology, economics, and other disciplines describe agents as systems driven by beliefs and desires. This post argues that the belief-desire view can be derived from classic theorems from optimal control and reinforcement learning. This suggests seeing beliefs and desires as properties of optimal policies rather than as assumptions from folk psychology.</span></i></p><h1><span>Introduction</span></h1><p><span>One way to think about agents is as "</span><i><span>systems that</span></i><span> </span><i><span>act for reasons"</span></i><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrhaa6iom3l9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrhaa6iom3l9">[1]</a></sup></span><span> This compact statement can be interpreted as encapsulating two key implications:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>The notion of </span><i><span>action</span></i><span> assumes a </span><b><span>boundary</span></b><span> between agent and environment, so that the former can act on the latter.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The term </span><i><span>reason </span></i><span>captures two kinds of internal activity: </span><b><span>motivations</span></b><span> associated with how to achieve specific goals or outcomes, and </span><b><span>beliefs</span></b><span> regarding what the agent infers to be the current state of affairs.</span></li></ul><p><span>In other words, an agent is a well-differentiated system that acts based on beliefs and desires. This view is compatible with perspectives that have been developed by various disciplines:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><a href="https://psycnet.apa.org/record/1932-01679-000" rel="noreferrer"><span>Behavioural science</span></a><span>, which sees agency as goal-directed behaviour.</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://www.taylorfrancis.com/books/mono/10.4324/9780429498619/notes-theory-choice-david-kreps" rel="noreferrer"><span>Economics</span></a><span>, which treats agency as the ability to select policies to achieve an objective.</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/An_Introduction_to_Cybernetics" rel="noreferrer"><span>Cybernetics</span></a><span>, which conceptualises agency as the ability to regulate the environment and keep it within a desired subset of states.</span></li></ol><p><span>Instead of taking the working definition</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9o51dagvz1h"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9o51dagvz1h">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><i><span>agency = beliefs + desires</span></i><span> </span></p><p><span>at face value, in this post I will discuss conceptual tools that can allow us </span><b><span>to derive this construction from formal considerations</span></b><span>. In particular, I will discuss the </span><b><span>separation principle</span></b><i><span>, </span></i><span>which I believe has potential to provide a rigorous foundation for this</span><i><span> </span></i><span>conceptualisation. These ideas take inspiration from </span><i><span>cybernetics</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>computational mechanics</span></i><span>, but distinctly leverage classic results in </span><i><span>optimal control theory</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>reinforcement learning</span></i><span>.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><b><span>Motivation — cybernetics.</span></b></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span>A related line of thinking pertains to the (in)famous </span><i><span>Good Regulator Theorem.</span></i><span> This result, put forward by cyberneticists </span><a href="https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1080/00207727008920220" rel="noreferrer"><span>Conant and Ashby</span></a><span> in 1970, suggests that an effective controller requires an internal model of what is being controlled. This makes intuitive sense: it is hard to predict a system that follows an intricate internal mechanism, and perhaps the only way to effectively control it is by understanding it.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 30.844033232628398%;"><img alt="Screenshot 2026-08-30 at 20.14.24.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788117268/lexical_client_uploads/mscegjtrdpqpzcj2smwk.png" /></figure><p><span>Unfortunately, the original paper has been both a source of inspiration and confusion. Lots have been written trying to clarify it; to read more, see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Dx9LoqsEh3gHNJMDk/fixing-the-good-regulator-theorem" rel="noreferrer"><span>this post</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JQefBJDHG6Wgffw6T/a-straightforward-explanation-of-the-good-regulator-theorem" rel="noreferrer"><span>this post</span></a><span>, and also </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.06326" rel="noreferrer"><span>this manuscript</span></a><span>. This post will follow these ideas in spirit, but not the formalisation as proposed there.</span></p></div></details><details class="detailsBlock"><p><b><span>Motivation — computational mechanics.</span></b></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span>Another related line of work is </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/XwDWNsk6BDRHvHxqE" rel="noreferrer"><span>computational mechanics</span></a><span>, which combines principles of information theory, theoretical computer science, and some sprinkles of statistical mechanics to describe the dynamics of prediction. Computational mechanics derives minimal structures required for optimal prediction, which take form in the</span><a href="https://link.springer.com/article/10.1023/A:1010388907793" rel="noreferrer"><span> </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-machine</span></a><span> and</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1412.2690" rel="noreferrer"><span> </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-transducer</span></a><span>. These ideas offer a rigorous explanation to (i) why optimal prediction requires specific computations, and (ii) what properties those computations need to satisfy. To read more, see </span><a href="https://bactra.org/thesis/" rel="noreferrer"><span>this thesis</span></a><span> or </span><a href="https://pubs.aip.org/aip/cha/article/28/3/033115/684965" rel="noreferrer"><span>this paper</span></a><span>.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 62.889444864048336%;"><img alt="Screenshot 2026-08-30 at 20.18.07.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788117491/lexical_client_uploads/deqmbr3airhk9xxzqs7o.png" /><figcaption><p><span>Figure adapted from Crutchfield &amp; Feldman, </span><i><span>Regularities unseen, randomness observed: levels of entropy convergence</span></i><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/cond-mat/0102181" rel="noreferrer"><span>arXiv:cond-mat/0102181</span></a></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>More recently, computational mechanics is providing effective tools to study the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/gTZ2SxesbHckJ3CkF/transformers-represent-belief-state-geometry-in-their" rel="noreferrer"><span>internal representations in deep learning</span></a><span>. Moreover, while originally formulated to study prediction, computational mechanics also provides a promising foundation for </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/L6Z6K8qXJhrSNMN4L/ai-in-a-vat-fundamental-limits-of-efficient-world-modelling" rel="noreferrer"><span>understanding perception-action loops</span></a><span>. The ideas developed here are heavily influenced by both the concepts and formalism of computational mechanics.</span></p></div></details><h1><span>What is a separation principle?</span></h1><p><span>Let's start by discussing what </span><i><span>separation principles</span></i><span> are, in general. </span></p><p><span>As a first approximation, consider a situation where we want to minimise the function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. A direct calculation shows that</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>Hence, solving the problem for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> separately does not yield the solution for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Put simply, one </span><i><span>does not</span></i><span> solve the problem by solving its parts separately.</span></p><p><span>A separation principle takes place when the divide-and-conquer strategy actually works, so one can solve a complicated optimisation problem by optimising various subcomponents separately. Concretely, a </span><i><span>separation theorem</span></i><b><span> </span></b><span>guarantees that optimal performance can be achieved by solving sub-tasks in isolation.</span></p><p><span>Let me further illustrate this idea by presenting an important separation theorem at the core of information theory. </span></p><p><span>The communication problem studied by information theory is about how to send bits from source to destination without error. The tools that a communication engineer employs to face this problem are two types of coding techniques: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><i><span>Source coding</span></i><span>, which compresses the original signal by removing redundancy in order to transmit as few bits as necessary.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefyk471nk3pyd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnyk471nk3pyd">[3]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><i><span>Channel coding</span></i><span> corresponds to error-correcting codes, which adds redundancy to protect the information bits from noise.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefz08b8uixc3j"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnz08b8uixc3j">[4]</a></sup></span></li></ul><img alt="Screenshot 2026-08-31 at 11.19.06.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788171555/lexical_client_uploads/dupgyoqqhzi3zx8t6hfu.png" /><p><span>Most people have heard of the following results by Claude Shannon: </span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>The </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shannon%27s_source_coding_theorem" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>source coding theorem</span></i></a><span>, which states that the shortest achievable average code length is given by the entropy of the source.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noisy-channel_coding_theorem" rel="noreferrer"><span>noisy-channel coding theorem</span></a><span>, which states that the maximal transmission rate that allows arbitrarily low decoding error is given by the mutual information between input and output symbols (maximised over input distributions).</span></li></ol><p><span>However, these results would not be as useful in the absence of the third, less known result known as the </span><b><span>source-channel separation theorem</span></b><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefh7eszt9asu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnh7eszt9asu">[5]</a></sup></span><span>  This result states that, under relatively mild conditions,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwz33uc2bbq9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwz33uc2bbq9">[6]</a></sup></span><span> an optimal solution for the overall communication problem can be achieved by breaking the problem into two separate parts: compression and error correction. This means that an optimal communication strategy can be designed by a team separately working on compression and another working on error-correction, without requiring coordination between them. Needless to say, this makes the problem much more tractable! </span></p><h1><span>The inference-control separation principle</span></h1><p><span>Let us now review results from optimal control theory and reinforcement learning that provide conditions for separating inference and control. In doing this, I will use the non-standard term </span><b><span>inference-control separation principle</span></b><span> to group together results that are often presented independently of each other, despite having a lot in common.</span></p><h2><span>Separation principle in optimal control theory</span></h2><p><span>Let us consider the classic instantiation of separation in the context of optimal control theory. For this, consider a control setting made by three components:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>a system to be controlled, whose state is described by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>,</span></li><li value="2"><span>the measurements an agent has access to, described by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and</span></li><li value="3"><span>the actions </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></li></ul><p><span>For simplicity, we assume that the system evolves dynamically over discrete time </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> via linear dynamics and noisy observations that can be described as</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span><span>,</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span><span>,</span></p><p><span>where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> are matrices, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the system's initial condition, and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> are independent white Gaussian noise terms. </span></p><img alt="fig0_lqg_regulator_tight.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788122317/lexical_client_uploads/axpeyfyjbhxdes93ivps.png" /><p><span>The action </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is determined according to a policy that has no direct access to the state </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, but only to the sequence of measurements </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Thus, we take </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> to be a deterministic function of the sequence of past measurements and actions </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. The controller aims to minimise the quadratic cost</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span><span>,</span></p><p><span>where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> are matrices and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the length of the time window over which the optimisation takes place. This is known as the discrete-time </span><b><span>LQG problem</span></b><span>, as it involves </span><b><span>L</span></b><span>inear dynamics, a </span><b><span>Q</span></b><span>uadratic loss function, and </span><b><span>G</span></b><span>aussian variables — being the one setting where everything is exactly solvable.</span></p><p><b><span>Separation principle —</span></b><span> To understand the solution of this problem, let us first consider the case of full observability, where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. In this setting, the optimal controller can be shown to only depend on the current state of the system, and the optimal solution </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> can be expressed as</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span><span>,</span></p><p><span>where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the "optimal gain" matrix (whose formula can be found in </span><a href="https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.5555/79089" rel="noreferrer"><span>standard textbooks</span></a><span>). So, if the controller can directly see the state of the system, this directly tells what the optimal action should be.</span></p><p><span>What happens with partial observability? In this setting, the optimal controller </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> can be written as</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span><span>,</span></p><p><span>where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the same as above and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is an estimation of the state of the system given by </span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>In fact, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the result of </span><i><span>Kalman filtering</span></i><span>, which is how recursive </span><i><span>Bayesian filtering</span></i><span> looks like under Gaussian dynamics.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvynbb3vfxfl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvynbb3vfxfl">[7]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>In summary, the optimal policy can be described as "estimate first, and then act as if your estimate were the actual state of the system". Thus, a </span><i><span>separation theorem</span></i><span> is taking place separating inference and control.</span></p><h2><span>Separation principle in reinforcement learning</span></h2><p><span>To understand what the separation principle looks like in reinforcement learning, let us slightly modify the nomenclature and notation:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>the state of the system to be controlled is described by a discrete variable </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>,</span></li><li value="2"><span>the measurements that the agent has access to are described by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and</span></li><li value="3"><span>the actions are denoted by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></li></ul><p><span>Let's assume that the variables follow a partially observed Markov decision process (POMDP), so that the environment dynamics are Markovian conditioned on the agent's action via a Markov kernel </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffcxsxt3z5u"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfcxsxt3z5u">[8]</a></sup></span><span> and the current observation depends on the current state of environment via a Markov kernel </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. The agent takes actions </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> following a policy </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> that maps sequences of previous actions and observations </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> into distribution of next actions. </span></p><p><span>In this setting, the probability of observing a sequence of observations and actions is </span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span></p><p><span>where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is a prior distribution over the environment's initial state. This is roughly the same as the control scenario studied above, but considering general discrete variables instead of Gaussian ones. </span></p><img alt="fig0b_pomdp_agent.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788122477/lexical_client_uploads/q13pcv5gsrrdhj79ms7e.png" /><p><span>Instead of a loss function, consider a reward </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> that the agent tries to maximise. More specifically, let us define the discounted future reward</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span><span>,</span></p><p><span>where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the so-called discount factor. Then, the goal of the agent is to find a policy </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> that maximises </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><b><span>Separation principle</span></b><span> — To solve this POMDP, let us first consider the Bayesian beliefs about the latent state </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. There are two ways to understand Bayesian beliefs:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>As random variables given by the conditional expectation </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, where the trajectory </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is regarded as a random variable.</span></li><li value="2"><span>As probability distributions </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> given by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, which correspond to the above expectation when conditioned on a specific realisation of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></li></ul><p><span>Bayesian beliefs have two key properties. First, as random variables, they contain all the information in </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> that is useful for predicting </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (i.e., they are sufficient statistics). Second, as distributions, they can be efficiently updated via</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span><span>,</span></p><p><span>where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is a deterministic function, and hence the dynamics of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> are Markovian conditioned on </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (as they only depend on </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>). These facts can be used to build a new Markov decision process (MDP), known as </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partially_observable_Markov_decision_process#Belief_MDP" rel="noreferrer"><b><span>belief MDP</span></b></a><span>, defined by the following variables:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>the states are the beliefs </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> of the latent state of the POMDP,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefy13b832pxyr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fny13b832pxyr">[9]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span>actions are </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (same as in the POMDP),</span></li><li value="3"><span>the reward function is </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></li></ul><p><span>In contrast with the original problem, this MDP is fully observable (as the agent knows its own beliefs), and therefore can be solved using standard RL techniques. The crucial point is that combining the estimation of Bayesian beliefs with the optimal solution for the belief MDP gives rise to a solution of the full POMDP.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwpafx067anl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwpafx067anl">[10]</a></sup></span><span> Thus, a </span><i><span>separation principle </span></i><span>is at play separating inference (calculating beliefs) and control (solving the belief MDP).</span></p><h2><span>Interim summary</span></h2><p><span>Both results in LQG control and POMDPs give rise to the same solution: they show that the optimal controller for a partially observed system can be built by</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>first performing optimal prediction over the latent state of the system, and then</span></li><li value="2"><span>solving the control problem using that prediction as a state.</span></li></ol><p><span>To further understand how the LQG and POMDP settings relate, let's discuss the shape of beliefs and the effect of actions (not essential for the rest of the post).</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><b><span>Beliefs are distributions, not point estimates.</span></b><span> </span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span>In the LQG setting, the optimal controller does not need the full distribution of the prediction — the prediction is a Gaussian posterior, but the controller only uses the mean and not the variance. This property is known as </span><a href="https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/abstract/document/1102781/" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>certainty equivalence</span></i></a><span>, which suggests to act as if a point estimate were the true state. </span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 83.98791540785498%;"><img alt="fig2_lqg_vs_pomdp_interface_v3.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788122005/lexical_client_uploads/rgiyudnfyg4c6sq70c91.png" /></figure><p><span>It would be tempting to describe beliefs as "best guesses" (e.g. maximum a posteriori), but certainty equivalence only holds because of the friendly properties of the LQG setup — a more general setting would not allow for this simplification. </span></p><p><span>In the more general POMDP case, the agent must carry the full belief distribution, not a point estimate. Nonetheless, separation survives in the Bayesian filter + belief-MDP form. Thus, "beliefs" in the agency sense are not mere best guesses but the full posterior, and only under special conditions do they collapse to a point estimate. More generally, the separation principle suggests to think of an agent's epistemic state not as what it thinks is true, but how its uncertainty is shaped.</span></p></div></details><details class="detailsBlock"><p><b><span>The dual effect.</span></b></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span>Actions do two things at once: they change the state of the system (</span><i><span>control effect</span></i><span>) and also change what the agent will subsequently be able to observe and thus learn (</span><i><span>informational effect</span></i><span>). Think of a robot with a noisy range sensor deciding whether to drive straight toward a goal or take a detour past a landmark: the detour is suboptimal for the control objective in isolation, but it sharpens the robot's position estimate, which improves every subsequent decision. This second channel — action shaping future belief — is what control theorists call </span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S1367578817301232" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>dual effect</span></i></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Part of why LQG separation is so clean is that while the evolution of the Kalman mean estimate depends on past actions and observations, its covariance does not. This means that the agent's </span><i><span>uncertainty</span></i><span> about the state at any future time is fixed the moment the problem is specified, as no action-observation sequence can make it larger or smaller. This implies that actions have no informational effect: there is no possible epistemic bonus that can reduce uncertainty, no matter what the agent does. This also explains why certainty equivalence holds: given that the covariance of the posterior evolves in a predetermined manner, it carries no useful information for the controller. </span></p><p><span>This simplification does not hold in more general POMDP settings, where actions can influence future information as well as the environment. In the belief MDP construction, the "state" is the belief </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and the transition kernel </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> captures </span><i><span>both</span></i><span> effects at once: how the action moves the underlying environment </span><i><span>and</span></i><span> how it reshapes the posterior.</span></p></div></details><h1><span>Implications</span></h1><p><span>Let us now return to the question we started with: can the working definition</span></p><p><i><span>agency = beliefs + desires</span></i></p><p><span>be derived, rather than assumed? The separation theorems reviewed above suggest that, at least in a specific sense, the answer is </span><i><span>yes</span></i><span>.</span></p><h2><span>Beliefs and desires as properties of solutions</span></h2><p><span>Let's recapitulate what the separation principle delivers. We posed a single, monolithic optimisation problem: find a policy </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> mapping histories into actions that minimise/maximise loss/reward. Nothing in this problem statement mentions beliefs, inference, or internal representations — the problem is stated in terms of pure behaviour. Yet, the solution naturally </span><i><span>factorises into sub-components.</span></i><span> Indeed, the above results state that the optimal policy can always be realised as the composition of two blocks:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>An </span><b><span>inference module</span></b><span>, which compresses the history </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> into a Bayesian belief about the latent state that can be recursively updated. This module knows about dynamics but is oblivious about loss/rewards.</span></li><li value="2"><span>A </span><b><span>control module</span></b><span>, which selects actions as a function of that belief alone. This module knows about loss/rewards but not about raw observations. </span></li></ul><p><span>The interface between them is exactly the belief state, which is the output of the inference module and the input of the control module. Thus, the inference module can be interpreted as "where beliefs are made"; similarly, the control module can be interpreted as "where desires take place", as it is the only part of the policy that is directed by the reward/loss function. In this way, beliefs and desires</span><i><span> </span></i><span>are recovered not as assumptions about agents</span><i><span> </span></i><span>but as </span><i><span>properties of solutions.</span></i></p><img alt="fig1_inference_control_factorisation_v2.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788121965/lexical_client_uploads/iesf3qp0zfmeygp0ouaz.png" /><p><span>This is worth pausing on. The folk-psychological notions of </span><i><span>belief</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>desire</span></i><span> have been with us at least since Aristotle, and they are usually treated as primitives — as the ingredients one starts with when theorising about minds. What the separation principle offers is something different: not a definition of these terms but a </span><i><span>derivation</span></i><span> of them. Beliefs and desires appear here not because we built them in, but because the optimal solution to a problem stated in purely behavioural terms turns out to have that shape. Whether or not one takes folk psychology seriously as a theory of mind, this is at least a reason to think its central distinction is not arbitrary. </span></p><p><span>It is remarkable that this modularity arises from solving a single, monolithic optimisation problem. We have seen this shape before: it is precisely the payoff that made the source-channel separation theorem so valuable! There, an optimal communication system could be designed by a compression team and an error-correction team working independently, without coordination — the source coder needed to know nothing about the channel, and the channel coder needed to know nothing about the source. </span></p><p><span>The inference-control separation delivers the exact analogue for agency, conferring a form of </span><i><span>compositional generalisation. </span></i><span>Indeed, if the reward changes but the environment does not, only the control module needs to be updated; if the environment shifts but the goals remain, only the inference module needs revising. Thus, the two components can be developed, improved, and swapped independently.</span></p><h2><span>Agents as cognitive light-cones</span></h2><p><span>These results may also provide some formal ground to conceiving of agents as </span><i><span>cognitive light-cones</span></i><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0mlo12tbbnxh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0mlo12tbbnxh">[11]</a></sup></span><span> The idea goes as follows: an agent is a system whose behaviour is both sensitive to what it can remember about the past (the past component of the cone) and what it can foresee (the future component of the cone).</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 90.31816465256797%;"><img alt="asd.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788081057/lexical_client_uploads/a0n95xwqtvk451ngqzg5.png" /><figcaption><p><span>Figure from Levin (2019), </span><a href="https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/psychology/articles/10.3389/fpsyg.2019.02688/full" rel="noreferrer"><span>Frontiers in psychology, 10, 2688</span></a><span>.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>This idea suggests an operational way to assess the capabilities of an agent: measure the size of its light-cone. This could be done using tools from computational mechanics — for example, studying how deep into the past causal states go, and doing the same for retrodictive causal states (which build sufficient statistics from future to past).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefszaak3szcx"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnszaak3szcx">[12]</a></sup></span></p><h2><span>The separation principle is normative, not descriptive</span></h2><p><span>Please note that the separation principle says that </span><i><span>an optimal agent</span></i><span> can be built as inference + control, but it does not say that any given agent </span><i><span>is</span></i><span> built this way. In particular, a policy trained end-to-end by gradient descent is under no obligation to organise itself into a filter feeding a planner.</span></p><p><span>But the separation principle gives us something subtler: it tells us that the belief state is a </span><i><span>sufficient statistic</span></i><span> for optimal behaviour. This suggests that any agent approaching optimality must be computing something as informative as the Bayesian posterior (whether or not it is legible as such). In this way, the separation principle turns into a lens for interpretability: we should expect belief-like structure inside competent agents, and we can go looking for it. Recent work from colleagues at </span><a href="https://www.simplexaisafety.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Simplex</span></a><span> in finding </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/gTZ2SxesbHckJ3CkF/transformers-represent-belief-state-geometry-in-their" rel="noreferrer"><span>belief-state geometry in the residual streams of transformers</span></a><span> is, I think, an early vindication of this expectation. At </span><a href="https://xorlabs.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span>XOR Labs</span></a><span> we are currently investigating to what degree these results transfer to deep RL agents trained to do control tasks.</span></p><p><span>At this point, it is useful to distinguish two separate concerns:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Control problems / POMDPs can have multiple optimal policies. In these settings, the separation principle states that there exists one optimal policy that can be factored, but doesn't guarantee all do.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1mbfaaocgqk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1mbfaaocgqk">[13]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span>When only one optimal solution exists, the separation principle says this unique behaviour </span><i><span>admits</span></i><span> a realisation as filter + belief-controller — but it does not say that's the only realisation. Indeed, the same mapping from histories to next action can be implemented in different ways; for instance, in a finite setting the mapping can always be implemented as a large look-up table.</span></li></ul><p><span>Regarding the second concern, there are various pieces of evidence suggesting that deep learning architectures have an implicit bias for simplicity, which makes them find simpler implementations of algorithms whenever they exist.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefarsryj9ko0i"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnarsryj9ko0i">[14]</a></sup></span><span> Therefore, while it is often </span><i><span>possible </span></i><span>to implement a policy via a gigantic look-up table, there are reasons to believe that policies obtained via deep learning techniques will tend towards factorised ones.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefw1yg7onle3e"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnw1yg7onle3e">[15]</a></sup></span></p><p><b><span>Where does separation fail?</span></b><span> As Shannon's </span><i><span>source-channel separation theorem</span></i><span>, the </span><i><span>inference-control separation theorems</span></i><span> hold under fairly general conditions — not relying on finite alphabets or stationarity. That said, for it to be useful one needs to assume that the agents under consideration have enough compute to build (quasi-)optimal policies. Under bounded rationality, model misspecification, or multi-agent interaction, the clean factorisation can break: what to represent starts depending on what you want, and inference becomes goal-driven.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefaugauoznnu5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnaugauoznnu5">[16]</a></sup></span><span> Many of the most interesting aspects of agency may come from the ways in which real agents deviate from the separation principle — but that is a topic for a future post.</span></p><h2><span>Coda</span></h2><p><span>Before concluding, let me ask: does the separation principle derive beliefs and desires from first principles? </span></p><p><span>Concretely, the separation principle offers the following:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>It reveals that optimal policies can be factored into two separate modules.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The signal shared between the modules can be interpreted as beliefs, so that the first (inference) module can be interpreted as "where beliefs are made". </span></li><li value="3"><span>The second (control) module can be interpreted as "where desires take place", as it is the only part of the policy that is directed by the reward/loss function.</span></li></ol><p><span>Thus, the separation principle provides formal ground for articulating the view of agents as "systems that act for reasons", displaying behaviour that is sensitive to both the past (what it remembers as part of its beliefs) and the future (due to foreseeing and planning to achieve goals).</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 67.4612915407855%;"><img alt="Screenshot 2026-08-30 at 23.56.45.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788130614/lexical_client_uploads/tnmdv3gc7tc1jqk08lrf.png" /></figure><p><span>However, one has to acknowledge that the separation principle assumes an optimisation problem which already contains a reward or cost function. Following this reasoning, one could argue that the desire or preference has not been really derived, as it has been baked in externally. More precisely, one can interpret the separation principle not as deriving</span><i><span> why desires exist in the first place</span></i><span>, but as explaining why they can be treated as separate entities from beliefs.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0xuand9iyhar"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0xuand9iyhar">[17]</a></sup></span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrhaa6iom3l9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrhaa6iom3l9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>There is a huge literature discussing various views on what agency is; if you want to read more, perhaps take a look into </span><a href="https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/knowledge-engineering-review/article/abs/intelligent-agents-theory-and-practice/CF2A6AAEEA1DBD486EF019F6217F1597" rel="noreferrer"><span>this</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0004370223001091" rel="noreferrer"><span>this paper</span></a><span> and references therein.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9o51dagvz1h"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9o51dagvz1h">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This post will take the boundary between agent and environment as given to focus on their internal activity. To read about boundaries, see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/LWJsgNYE8wzv49yEc" rel="noreferrer"><span>these posts</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnyk471nk3pyd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefyk471nk3pyd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div this result doesn't have a wikipedia page...</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnwz33uc2bbq9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a post.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn1mbfaaocgqk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1mbfaaocgqk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This is a real issue, but can be disregarded in some settings. For instance, "almost every" finite POMDP has a single optimal solution. Multiple optimal solutions in POMDPs are caused, for example, by ties in the optimal Q-function, which are broken by arbitrarily small perturbations on the reward.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnarsryj9ko0i"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefarsryj9ko0i">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>There have been numerous discussions about the simplicity biases of deep learning, see for example </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Dw8mskAvBX37MxvXo/deep-learning-as-program-synthesis-1" rel="noreferrer"><span>this post</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnw1yg7onle3e"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefw1yg7onle3e">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For example, we have evidence that transformers find factored representations whenever they are available — see </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02385" rel="noreferrer"><span>this paper</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnaugauoznnu5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefaugauoznnu5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This is arguably where phenomena like motivated reasoning and wishful thinking become rational responses to bounded resources rather than mere biases. To read more, see </span><a href="https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257/0002828054825493" rel="noreferrer"><span>this</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/behavioral-and-brain-sciences/article/abs/resourcerational-analysis-understanding-human-cognition-as-the-optimal-use-of-limited-computational-resources/586866D9AD1D1EA7A1EECE217D392F4A" rel="noreferrer"><span>this</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0xuand9iyhar"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0xuand9iyhar">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For answering the </span><i><span>why</span></i><span> question, one may need to consider arguments based on natural selection, or perhaps approaches to agency based on the enactive tradition — as developed, for example, in </span><a href="https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/1059712309343819" rel="noreferrer"><span>this paper</span></a><span>. See related discussions questioning utility functions in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ALmBydH53DE3dSzCh/utilities-as-legendre-duals-of-probabilities" rel="noreferrer"><span>this post</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/awMDNhoL6J97s6wFJ/the-separation-principle-where-do-beliefs-and-desires-come#comments">Discuss</a>

## Engineering

### [Making Your Data Ready for Agentic AI](https://martinfowler.com/articles/making-data-ready-for-agentic-ai.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/making-data-ready-for-agentic-ai.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/making-data-ready-for-agentic-ai/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Lots of organizations are excited about what AI can do to streamline
      their processes, save money, and juice margins. But AI's capabilities are
      founded on the data that AI accesses, and for many organizations that
      foundation is little more than sand. <b class="author">Pramod Sadalage</b> and
      <b class="author">Prem Chandrasekaran</b> write about how to build a reliable
      foundation of data that can be accurate and trusted.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/making-data-ready-for-agentic-ai.html">more…</a></p>

### [Fragments: August 24](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-08-24.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>I was listening to <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/08/18/opinion/ezra-klein-podcast-helen-toner.html">Ezra Klein’s interview with Helen Toner</a> about the recent OpenAI hack of Hugging Face and the subsequent discovery that there were swarms of agents inside OpenAI doing unsanctioned activities. One of the points Klein made was that at no point did any of these (thousands of?) agents ever try to check in with a human</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>[Klein:] So these message boards — you have however many A.I. agents posting hundreds of thousands of messages. At no point do they say: Hey, researchers, programmers, parents at OpenAI, Anthropic — do you want us coordinating with each other on this message board we have created in the innards of your systems?</p>
</blockquote>

<blockquote>
  <p>[Toner:] Or even F.Y.I., we have a message board we’re coordinating on in the innards of your system.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Listening to that, another thing occurred to me - <em>none of these agents thought to rat the others out</em>. No “hey, some of the agents in here are doing sketchy things”, no sign of an AI whistleblower.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Is the AI bubble so big that the frontier companies like OpenAI and Anthropic have no way of becoming a viable business? If that’s the case, Bruce Schneier and Nathan Sanders <a href="https://www.schneier.com/blog/archives/2026/08/if-the-markets-reject-openai-and-anthropic-the-us-should-nationalize-them.html">have a possible path:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Evidence suggests the market itself could reassess that these companies offer nothing of financial value. In that case, perhaps we can return them both to their original purposes. If these AI companies should fail in the financial markets, the US should nationalize them and convert them into national labs operated under democratic control that preserve their benefit to the public interest.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Such an idea may strike many people, used to the laissez-faire free enterprise world of Silicon Valley, as sacrilege, disaster, even socialism. But the United States made world-beating technological progress through such institutions in the recent past. AT&amp;T was a quasi-government entity that led the world in telecommunications and electronics after the second world war.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The US has a long, successful history of these kinds of institutions, which have produced world-shaping innovations in spaceflight, telecommunications, nuclear power and more. Congress currently manages a $200bn R&amp;D portfolio, within which frontier AI development is, arguably, a glaring gap.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Here’s a message for those readers who live in Massachusetts, just to the north of me, specifically in congressional district MA-06. I don’t usually endorse political candidates, but I’ve made an exception for <a href="https://bethfordemocracy.com/">Beth Anders-Beck</a>, who is running for that house district. I’ve known Beth for many years and have a high opinion of her smarts, wisdom, and compassion. They would make an excellent member of congress.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Kevlin Henney posts “one weird trick” for <a href="https://kevlinhenney.medium.com/streamline-your-linkedin-experience-with-this-one-weird-trick-041daa16777a">deciding when to skip reading LinkedIn posts</a>, essentially by identifying a common pattern for skippable posts:</p>

<ol>
  <li>Post is too long</li>
  <li>Contains a (crummy) info-graphic</li>
  <li>No voice of poster (instead “aspiring anodyne anonymity”</li>
</ol>

<p>It seems like a good approach. I, however, have a simpler one - skip all LinkedIn posts.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Bartosz Ocytko has detailed and thoughtful post about the usage of <a href="https://engineering.zalando.com/posts/2026/08/agentic-engineering-at-zalando-a-snapshot.html">agentic programming at Zalando</a>. Like most companies I hear from, they are convinced of the value of agentic programming but still exploring how best to do it. One notable step they’ve taken is building platforms to act as a clear portal for API access and tools to support chat UI and CLI. This allows them better support good security practices and to monitor usage of models.</p>

<p>They have seen signs of agentic programming increasing the complexity of codebases, including leading to larger commit messages.</p>

<p>The write-up spends a lot of time on knowledge sharing, how to pass on skills, and the support of experiments.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>With &gt;200 teams innovating and broadly exploring the ecosystem, the question arises whether and when to converge. We believe it’s way too early for this. While agentic engineering practices are still in their early stages, our key objective is transparency and exchange across teams.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I was struck by their use of an LLM to assess the risk of pull-requests. Those with  a low risk of rollout can be auto-approved, reducing lead time by 20-40%. An interesting consequence of this is that it encouraged folks to split pull-requests so low risk portions can take advantage of the fast approval. Any changes to configurations are automatically made high-risk, which they feel protects them from common outage traps.</p>

<p>They repeat the common thread that the value of AI depends greatly on underlying skills.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Like anyone in the industry we observe how AI amplifies the good and bad practices across our organization. Teams that get carried away with agentic engineering end up with large PRs that discourage reviewers and slow down delivery until a team adjusts their practices.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Julia Curlee was a senior intelligence official in the White House. She had served under administrations of both parties, been the briefer for Vice President Pence, and on the National Security Council under Biden. She writes an absorbing account of her relationship with Pence and shares observations about the changes to the intelligence community under the current administration, including <a href="https://www.theatlantic.com/magazine/2026/10/trump-white-house-transgender-mike-pence/688284/?gift=zGsHlQiVhVhk3cFVqv--gzIqOYZAxuFeY4CtPlqqfx4&amp;utm_source=copy-link&amp;utm_medium=social&amp;utm_campaign=share">recent events at the CIA</a> (gift link)</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The agency has been gutted as part of a deliberate plan, the director of the Office of Management and Budget once boasted, to put the people who defend our country “in trauma.” Analysts have been fired in public or questioned by the FBI; decade-old assessments have been denounced by the CIA director in the press. The president calls analysis “virtual treason” when it contradicts his preferred reality, and uses the CIA to undermine public confidence in American elections.</p>

  <p>Fear has done its work. Irreplaceable officers with crucial language and technical skills, and decades of experience, have walked out the door. Those who remain within an agency built to deliver hard truths are being muzzled.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>For a worthwhile sample of her analysis, read this evaluation of the <a href="https://www.lawfaremedia.org/article/fighting-while-talking--the-iran-war-enters-its-bargaining-phase">current bargaining between the US and Iran</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Most wars do not end in “unconditional surrender.” They end when both sides accept terms. Paul Pillar’s classic study of war termination, “Negotiating Peace,” treats combat and diplomacy as a single process: Each side fights to improve the terms it can demand at the table, and talks to lock in what the fighting has won.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>She continued to serve the second Trump administration even though they knew she was trans, until her position was made public.</p>

<p>Autocrats seem appealing, with the promise to get things done without the ponderous constraints of rule of law or bureaucratic procedure. There are occasional “Good Emperors” who raise people based on merit, but more often such power attracts corruption, nepotism, and toadies.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Flailing regimes dehumanize minorities to distract from their failures. When the economy collapses or a war goes badly, they find a tiny group of people, make them the enemy within, and rally the country against them. This is how it’s gone in Iran. Hungary. Russia. I wrote PDBs about it. This will not stop with trans people. It never has.</p>
</blockquote>

---

### [Citizens Build, Agents Execute, Experts Govern](https://martinfowler.com/rachels-ramblings/citizens-agents-experts.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p class="precis"><b>TL;DR</b><br /><i>Why building an app over the weekend isn't the same as building enterprise software</i></p>
<img src="https://martinfowler.com/rachels-ramblings/card.png" /><p>I’ve noticed an interesting gap opening up over the last six months. It isn’t really a gap in technology. It’s a gap in what different people think software engineering actually is.</p>

<p>The conversation usually starts the same way. A non-techie, maybe an executive, tells me about something they’ve built over the weekend. Sometimes it’s a chatbot. Sometimes it’s an internal workflow. Sometimes it’s a surprisingly polished application that solves a real business problem. They’re excited, and they should be. Twelve months ago they probably couldn’t have built it at all. Then comes the question.</p>

<p>“If AI can do this now, why aren’t our engineering teams delivering ten times faster?”</p>

<p>It’s a perfectly reasonable question, after all we’ve all seen the demos. The first thing that would come to my head is “you don’t know what it takes to build enterprise grade software”. But then I think about what I mean and how to explain it to a non-technical person without sounding super patronising. And then it hit me, we did this to ourselves. We’ve spent so many years banging on about how to write good software that everyone has assumed writing software is the same as software engineering.</p>

<p>The application someone builds over the weekend is real software. It likely solves a real problem or demonstrates an idea. Sometimes it’s genuinely impressive. I don’t want to diminish that because I think one of the most exciting things AI has done is dramatically increase the number of people who can turn ideas into working software. That’s cool, I totally get it. The first apps and “hello worlds” I ever built excited me enough to choose this as an actual career so the excitement is real and I don’t want to temper it too much.</p>

<p>But your first hello world, which these days can be an entire app with all kinds of features, is very, very (extra very on purpose) different from introducing software into a production environment in a highly regulated enterprise, as an example. But why?</p>

<p>The moment that application becomes something the business depends on, the questions change completely. Is customer data protected? What happens when a dependency fails? Can someone else understand this system in two years’ time? Will it survive an audit? Can it cope with a thousand times more users than it has today, what about millions in one day? How will we know something is wrong before our customers do? Those questions don’t show up in a demo or in the build phase at all unless an experienced engineer is in the room. I certainly wasn’t asking them when I was building my first apps. I only cared about features!</p>

<p>This is where experienced engineers become more important, not less. Not because they’re the only people who can build the software anymore, but because they have the judgement to know whether we can trust it: whether the design is good, the risks are understood, and the thing that works today won’t become somebody else’s nightmare six months from now.</p>

<p><a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/FutureOfSoftwareDevelopment.html">At FOSE</a> a few weeks ago, we spent surprisingly little time talking about coding. We talked about whether code was still the source of truth, and occasionally about how much we missed writing it, but mostly we talked about design, architecture, governance, learning and judgement. One team described spending the day designing a specification, letting agents work overnight and reviewing the results the next morning. The interesting bit for me wasn’t the overnight pipeline, cool as that was. It was what the humans were doing: deciding what good looked like, making trade-offs and judging whether what came back was actually what they wanted. We also kept coming back to good design, because it turns out that when agents can generate lots of code very quickly, good design matters more, not less.</p>

<p>That made me wonder whether we’ve been thinking about scarcity in the wrong way. We’ve spent decades optimising around people who can write code because they were scarce and expensive. I’m not convinced that was ever the real scarcity, but that’s probably another ramble. What feels scarce now is good engineering judgement: knowing what good looks like, understanding the risks and knowing when something that works is actually safe to trust in production. Because software doesn’t exist to be built. It exists to run in production and safely solve the problem it was created for. <strong>Organisations don’t run on code. They run on trust.</strong></p>

<p>A few months ago I found myself saying something in a conversation almost without thinking.</p>

<p><strong>Citizens build. Agents execute. Experts govern.</strong></p>

<p>It sounded cool and I thought marketing would like it, so I wrote it down. Then I left it alone for a while. The funny thing about writing these ramblings is that I don’t know whether I believe something until I’ve let it bounce around in my head for a while and also said it to other people I trust like senior engineers at Thoughtworks. Sometimes I come back convinced I was talking nonsense. Occasionally I realise there was something more interesting hiding underneath. This was one of those occasions where the latter was true.</p>

<p>At first I thought I was talking about roles. Citizens build software (essentially non-engineers). Agents write the code. Engineers become governors. But I don’t actually think that’s what I meant. I think I was talking about where value is moving. AI has given everyone a new way to express their ideas. The execution is increasingly handled by agents. They write the code, refactor it, generate tests, fix bugs and iterate at a speed that simply wasn’t possible before. But neither of those things reduces the need for expertise.</p>

<p>In fact, I think it does exactly the opposite. When everyone can create software, somebody still has to decide whether that software deserves to exist inside an enterprise system in PRODUCTION. Somebody still has to think about architecture. Security. Resilience. Operability. Compliance. Cost. The boring stuff that nobody gets excited about in a demo but that becomes painfully important the first time a customer can’t log in or an auditor comes knocking.</p>

<p>That’s why I don’t think experienced engineers become less important. I think they become dramatically more leveraged. Their job shifts from building every feature themselves to creating the environment in which thousands of features can be built safely by other people and by agents. They become the people who design the guardrails, the platforms, the engineering practices and the feedback loops that allow everyone else to move quickly without creating chaos.</p>

<p>Perhaps that’s the future software organisation. Not one where everyone becomes a software engineer. Not one where software engineers disappear. One where almost anyone can create software, agents increasingly execute it, and engineering expertise becomes the thing that allows all of that creativity to scale safely. And to be clear I do not mean people build stuff and throw it to engineers to fix, that is a total antipattern for another ramble.</p>

<p>Perhaps that’s why the executives and engineers I’ve been speaking to sometimes sound as though they’re describing completely different futures. The executive sees that anyone can now build software. The engineer sees that somebody still has to live with it. Both are right. They’re simply looking at different parts of the same system we have to solve to create whatever the future actually ends up being.</p>

---
